) follow from the usual (local) Cauchy-Lipschitz theory for ODEs.

∎

In the next subsections, we

prove that, for
N
sufficiently large,

u^{N,\nu}
is in fact a global solution over the entire interval
[0,T]
(in which case, it is also globally unique) and satisfies suitable regularity properties.

In particular, part of our effort
consists in obtaining bounds that are independent of
\nu
and
N

(at least for
N
large)
.

3.1.1.
The auxiliary HJ equations

The main difficulty we have to face in the study of (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

) (and in general of (

⁢
H

J
N

)) comes from the non-linear term
H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu})*D^{N}
. As the latter is also non-local, we cannot apply the usual approach for Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) equations.

We proceed as follows.
First, we introduce an auxiliary HJ equation in which the Hamiltonian
H
is replaced by a
new one, denoted by
\widetilde{H}
, whose properties (clarified later in this section) facilitate the analysis of the corresponding HJ equation. We refer to the solution of this auxiliary (non-local) HJ equation as
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. Subsequently, we establish certain (uniform in
\nu
) estimates for both
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
and
\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. To achieve this, it becomes necessary to introduce a secondary auxiliary HJ equation, which is
in fact the (usual) HJ equation arising in the mean field game system associated with
(\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{F}},\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{G}})
and
which is thus well-understood. Thereafter, we return from the secondary to the primary auxiliary HJ equations and infer
the desired regularity properties on
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. Finally, we show that we can tune
\widetilde{H}
appropriately in order to transfer all the results to the initial (non-local) HJ equation (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

) and its solution
u^{N,\nu}
.

We first explain the constraints we put on the new Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}
.
For an arbitrary
M>0
(we choose the value later, see Remark
3.10
), we are considering a continuously differentiable function
\widetilde{H}\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that:

i.

There exists a positive constant
C=C(M)
, independent of
x
, such that for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\big\lvert\widetilde{H}(x,p)\big\rvert\leq C(M)(\lvert p\rvert+1)
,

\big\lvert\mathrm{D}_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,p)\big\rvert\leq C(M)
and
\big\lvert\mathrm{D}_{p}\widetilde{H}(x,p)\big\rvert\leq C(M)
;

ii.

For any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\lvert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(x,p)\rvert\leq M
, it holds
\widetilde{H}(x,p)=H(x,p)
.

Such an
\widetilde{H}
can be easily constructed by multiplication of
H
by a cut-off function.
Importantly, we do not need
\widetilde{H}
to be convex. Indeed,
in the computations where the specific structure of
\widetilde{H}
plays a role,
we do not use any argument based on a stochastic control interpretation of the approximating HJ equation.
Instead, we always invoke standard estimates from the literature on non-degenerate semi-linear PDEs
(in which Hamiltonians can be quite general). These estimates allow us to prove that, for
N
large enough, the solution
to the approximating HJ equation driven by
\widetilde{H}
actually lives in the domain where

\widetilde{H}
and
H
coincide. This makes it possible to use convexity again for the rest of the analysis.

We can now state our primary auxiliary HJ equation: on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
,

(
\widetilde{HJ}_{N,\nu}
)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x)&=-\Delta\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x)+\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t})*D^{N}(x)-\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{t},\cdot)*D^{N}(x),\\
\quad\\
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{T}(x)&=\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\nu_{T},\cdot)*D^{N}(x).\end{cases}

For the moment, we focus on (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

) (and forget temporarily (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

)).
Only at the end of our analysis, we prove that, under certain conditions, the two equations actually coincide.

In order to deal with (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

), let us introduce a secondary auxiliary HJ equation:

(
\overline{HJ}_{\nu}
)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v^{\nu}_{t}(x)&=-\Delta v^{\nu}_{t}(x)+H(x,\nabla v^{\nu}_{t}(x))-\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{t},x)\quad\text{ in }[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\quad\\
v^{\nu}_{T}(x)&=\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\nu_{T},x)\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{T}^{d}.\end{cases}

Notice that the Hamiltonian in (

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

) is not the new
\widetilde{H}
, but the original
H
.
In particular,
(

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

) matches the usual HJ equation arising in mean field game/control theory
(see the backward equation in
(

⁢
F

B
∞

)).
In fact, we will show in Section
3.1.2

how to pass from the equation driven by
\widetilde{H}
to the equation driven by
{H}
and conversely, by
choosing
M
appropriately.

3.1.2.
Estimates on
v^{\nu}

We study the smoothness of
v^{\nu}
by using standard techniques for parabolic equations. We first notice
from the classical theory of Hamilton-Jacobi equations (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Theorem V.6.1]
)
that there exists a unique classical solution
v^{\nu}\in\mathrm{C}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
to (

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

). In the next proposition, we recall some (standard) regularity estimates on
v^{\nu}
. Even though these estimates are already known, we give a sketch of the proof for completeness.

Proposition 3.2
.

Under Assumption
2.2
,

v^{\nu}\in\mathrm{C}^{1,{q}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Moreover, there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
, independent of
M
and
\nu
, such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert v^{\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,q}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q).

Proof.

By a classical result on nonlinear parabolic equations (see e.g.
[
25
, Theorem V.3.1]
), there exists a constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H)
such that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\nabla v^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H)
. In particular, the bound is independent of
\nu
. This, together with the continuity of
H
, entails the (uniform in time) boundedness of the mapping
x\mapsto H(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))
. Denoting by
p=p_{t}(x)
the usual heat kernel on the
d
-dimensional torus, we have from
Duhamel’s formula:

\displaystyle\lvert v^{\nu}_{t}(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H)+\left\lvert\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{s-t}(x-y)H\left(y,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}(y)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H)+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert H\left(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}\right)\rVert_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H).

Here we exploited the uniform in
\mu
bounds on
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}
and
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}
to control
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{t},\cdot)
and
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\nu_{T},\cdot)
in (

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

).
Finally, regularity up to order
q
(with uniform in time bounds)
can be obtained
by using Lemma A.3 in
[
16
]
, thanks to
the smoothness of
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}
,
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}
, and
H
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Let us set
\overline{M}:=\max\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{\infty}_{t})\rVert_{\infty},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sup_{\nu}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{t})\rVert_{\infty}\}
. Notice

from Propositions
2.11

and

3.2
together with the continuity of
\mathrm{D}_{p}H
that
\overline{M}
is
bounded by a constant
C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
.
Consequently,
if
M
in the definition of
\widetilde{H}
is greater than
\overline{M}
, then it is guaranteed that
for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]
,
\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla u^{\infty}_{t}(x))=H(x,\nabla u^{\infty}_{t}(x))
and
\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla v^{\nu}_{t}(x))=H(x,\nabla v^{\nu}_{t}(x))
. This ensures that both
v^{\nu}
and
u^{\infty}
remain solutions of (

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

) and (

⁢
F

B
∞

), respectively, with
\widetilde{H}
replacing
H
. Furthermore, these solutions obviously retain the regularity properties outlined in Propositions
2.11
and
3.2
.

Remark 3.4
.

In Propositions
2.11
and
3.2
, we ask Assumpion
2.2
to be in force and thus
q
to be greater than or equal to
d+3
. This is to make the exposition simpler. In fact, the results hold for any choice of
q>2
, no matter the value of the dimension
d
.

3.1.3.
Estimates on
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}

We now address
the primary auxiliary equation (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

). Due to the non-local term
\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu})*D^{N}
, we cannot treat it by using Duhamel’s formula as simply as we did in the proof of
Proposition

3.2
. Instead,
our strategy is to combine the regularity properties established in
Proposition

3.2

with the convergence properties of
D^{N}
in order to establish similar properties on
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. A key role will be played by the Lipschitz property of
\widetilde{H}
in the
p
variable.

We observe that existence and uniqueness of a global classical solution
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}

(to (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

)) can be obtained as in Proposition
3.1
,
with the difference that Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem now applies in its global version because

\widetilde{H}
is globally Lipschitz.
Subsequently, we can concentrate ourselves on the regularity of
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. We first establish estimates in
L^{2}
norm. They will be crucial to prove the main estimates of this subsection, which are stated in
\mathrm{C}^{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}
norm.

We start with the following statement,
which is true whatever the value of
M
in the definition of
\widetilde{H}
(in particular there is no need to choose
M
according to Remark
3.3
).

Proposition 3.5
.

Under Assumption
2.2
, there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}+\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2})\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M).

Proof.

We integrate (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

) with respect to
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. By combining integration by parts and Lemma
A.3
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\nu_{T},\cdot)\rVert_{2}^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s},\cdot)*D^{N},\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N},\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T)(\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}\rVert^{2}_{\infty}+\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}\rVert^{2}_{\infty})+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C(M)^{2}}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2C(M)^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{C(M)^{2}}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,

where we used
\lvert\widetilde{H}(x,p)\rvert\leq C(M)(\lvert p\rvert+1)

in the penultimate line. Gronwall’s inequality leads to
\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)
.

To estimate
\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
, we can take the
x
-derivative of (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

), and then integrate the differentiated equation against
\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. We obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}](\nu_{T},\cdot)\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle\nabla[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}](\nu_{s},\cdot)*D^{N},\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle\nabla[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}],\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T)(\lVert\nabla[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}]\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\infty}^{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+C(M)\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,

and the claim is obtained as for
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
.
∎

From now on, let us consider
M\geq\overline{M}
, where
\overline{M}
is chosen as in Remark
3.3
. Thus we can
replace
\widetilde{H}
by
H
in
(

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

)
. We will refine again the requirements on
M
later on in this section.

Lemma 3.6
.

Under Assumption
2.2
, there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{t}-\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)}{N^{q-1}}.

Proof.

From (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

) and (

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{t}-\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}](\nu_{T},\cdot)-\nabla[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}](\nu_{T},\cdot)*D^{N}\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s},\cdot)-\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s},\cdot)*D^{N},\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N},\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s.

Lemma
A.5
allows us to control the
L^{2}
norms of
(\nabla\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}-\nabla\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}*D^{N})
and
(\nabla\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}-\nabla\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}*D^{N})
. Indeed, for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
we have that
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu,\cdot),\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\mu,\cdot)\in H^{{q}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with Sobolev norms that are independent of
\mu
. By combining this with Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, it follows

(3.3)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{t}

\displaystyle-\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{2}+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\left(\frac{1}{N^{{2q-2}}}+\frac{1}{N^{{2q}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Now, regarding the term
\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{2}
, we can first remove
D^{N}
(convolution by
D^{N}
is a contraction in
L^{2}
, see Lemma
A.3
) and then use the Lipschitz property of
\widetilde{H}
to obtain

\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{2}\leq C(M)\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}.

To control
\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{2}
, let us first show that the map

x\mapsto\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))
is of class
\mathrm{C}^{{q-1}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with a norm bounded by a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H)
. Indeed, since
M\geq\overline{M}
by assumption (see Remark
3.3
for the definition of
\overline{M}
), we have
\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))=H(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))
. Thus, the claim follows by recalling that
H\in\mathrm{C}^{{q}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
together with Proposition
3.2
. Finally, by Lemma
A.5
it follows that

\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H)}{N^{q-1}}.

By plugging these estimates in (
3.3
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{t}-\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\frac{1}{N^{{2q-2}}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+C(M)\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)\left(\frac{1}{N^{{2q-2}}}+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla^{2}v^{\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,

and so the result follows by Gronwall’s inequality.
∎

We now have all the ingredients to bound
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
and
\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
in
L^{\infty}
norm:

Proposition 3.7
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be in force. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
it holds:

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty})\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}),

for a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M,q)
.

And, there exist a positive constant

\widetilde{M}=\widetilde{M}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)

and an integer
\widetilde{N}=\widetilde{N}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
such that, for

M=\widetilde{M}
and
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, the constant
C
is independent of

M
.

Moreover, again
for
M=\widetilde{M}
and
N\geq\widetilde{N}
,
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}\in\mathrm{C}^{1,q}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
and

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q),

for a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
.

Remark 3.8
.

We emphasize that, differently from
u^{\infty}
and
v^{\nu}
in
Propositions
2.11
and
3.2
,

\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
cannot be estimated in
C^{q}
independently of
N
. Bounds are uniform in
N
up to the regularity order
\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor
. This is due to the presence of the non-local term in (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

).

Proof.

First Step.

We first establish the two bounds on
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
and its gradient.
With
p=p_{t}(x)
denoting the usual heat kernel on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
we deduce from Duhamel’s representation formula that, for every
(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{T-t}(x-y)\left[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\nu_{T},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle-\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{s-t}(x-y)\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{s-t}(x-y)\left[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s.

Then we have

(3.5)

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{s-t}

\displaystyle(x-y)\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg\rvert

(3.6)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert p_{s-t}(x-y)\rvert\left\lvert\left(\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\right)*D^{N}(y)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.7)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert p_{s-t}(x-y)\rvert\left\lvert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}(y)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s.

Regarding (
3.6
), Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Lemma
A.3
yield, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

(
3.6
)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t+\varepsilon}^{T}\lVert p_{s-t}\rVert_{2}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{t+\varepsilon}\lVert(\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}))*D^{N}\rVert_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t+\varepsilon}^{T}\lVert p_{s-t}\rVert_{2}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+C(d)N^{\frac{d}{2}}\int_{t}^{t+\varepsilon}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Then, we can combine Lemma
3.6
, the Lipschitz property of
\widetilde{H}
and the estimates for the heat kernel in Lemma
A.8
to get

(
3.6
)

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)\left(\frac{1}{N^{{q-1}}}\int_{t+\varepsilon}^{T}\frac{1}{(s-t)^{\frac{d}{4}}}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\varepsilon N^{\frac{d}{2}}}{N^{{q-1}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)\frac{\varepsilon^{-\frac{d}{4}+1}+\varepsilon N^{\frac{d}{2}}}{N^{{q-1}}}.

If we choose
\varepsilon=N^{-2}
, we have

\eqref{eqn: inftybound_1st}\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},M,H)}{N^{{q+1-\frac{d}{2}}}}.

We notice that the exponent in the denominator is positive because

q\geq d+3
.

For (
3.7
), we recall that the map
x\mapsto\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))
is of class
\mathrm{C}^{{q-1}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with a norm uniformly bounded by a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
. This is a consequence of Proposition
3.2
and the fact that
\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))=H(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))
for
M\geq\overline{M}
. Then, from Remark
A.7
,

\eqref{eqn: inftybound_2nd}\leq C(d,T,q)\int_{t}^{T}\left(\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{{\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}\rVert_{\infty,q-1}}}{N^{q-1-\frac{d}{2}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q),

where, to get the last constant, we used once again the fact that

q>\frac{d}{2}+1
.

Similarly, we have

\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{T-t}(x-y)\left[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\nu_{T},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\right\rvert\leq C(d,T,q)\left(\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{{\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}\rVert_{\infty,q}}}{N^{q-\frac{d}{2}}}\right),

and

\left\lvert\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{s-t}(x-y)\left[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rvert\leq C(d,T,q)\left(\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{{\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\infty,q}}}{N^{q-\frac{d}{2}}}\right).

Finally, by combining all these estimates with the Duhamel representation formula (
3.4
), we obtain

(3.8)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left(\frac{C_{1}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)}{N^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q-\frac{d}{2}}}}+C_{2}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\right)\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M,q).

Above, in (
3.8
), we introduced two different constants
\{C_{i}\}_{i=1,2}
in order to better keep track of the influence of the various parameters.

For the gradient, we can proceed similarly, by differentiating the equation solved by
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. Duhamel’s formula, combined with an integration by parts, gives for any
(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
:

(3.9)

\displaystyle\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{T-t}(x-y)\left[\nabla[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}](\nu_{T},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle-\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)\left[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s.

We proceed as for
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
. First,
following the derivation of
(
3.6
),
we get for any
\varepsilon>0
:

(3.10)

\begin{split}\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\nabla&p_{s-t}(x-y)\rvert\lvert(\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}))*D^{N}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)\rvert\lvert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\int_{t+\varepsilon}^{T}\lVert\nabla p_{s-t}\rVert_{2}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\quad+C(d)\int_{t}^{t+\varepsilon}\frac{N^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\sqrt{s-t}}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\quad+C(d,T,q)\int_{t}^{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{s-t}}\left(\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{{\lVert\nabla v^{\nu}_{s}\rVert_{\infty,q-1}}}{N^{q-1-\frac{d}{2}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M)}{N^{q-1}}\left(\varepsilon^{-\frac{d}{4}+\frac{1}{2}}+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{\frac{d}{2}}\right)+C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q),\end{split}

where we passed the term
1/N^{q-1-d/2}
appearing on the penultimate line into the last constant on the last line.
By taking
\varepsilon=N^{-2}
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rvert\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},M,H)}{N^{q-\frac{d}{2}}}+C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q).

Moreover, it holds

\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{T-t}(x-y)\left[\nabla[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}](\nu_{T},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\right\rvert\leq C(d,T,q)\left(\lVert\nabla\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{{\lVert\nabla\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}\rVert_{\infty,q-1}}}{N^{q-1-\frac{d}{2}}}\right),

and

\left\lvert\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)\left[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{s},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rvert\leq C(d,T,q)\left(\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{{\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\infty,q}}}{N^{q-\frac{d}{2}}}\right).

Thus, we deduce that

(3.11)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{C_{1}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M,q)}{N^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q-1-\frac{d}{2}}}}+C_{2}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,M,q).

Second Step.
We now address the second part of the statement.

To do so, we notice that it is possible to choose
M
and
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, for a certain
\widetilde{N}
depending only on
d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q
(see also Remark
3.10
below), such that the constants
C
in (
3.8
) and (
3.11
) become independent of
M
.

To make it clear, let us consider
\check{M}:=\sup_{\lvert p\rvert\leq C_{2}+1}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,p)\rVert_{\infty}
,
where
C_{2}:=C_{2}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
comes from the estimates above, and set
\widetilde{M}:=\max\{\check{M},\overline{M}\}
, where
\overline{M}:=\overline{M}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
is defined in Remark
3.3
. Then,
\widetilde{M}
depends only on the data
d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q
. In the definition of
\widetilde{H}
, we can choose
M=\widetilde{M}
. Then, we can take
\widetilde{N}:=\widetilde{N}(\widetilde{M})
, depending only on
d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q
, such that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{2}+1=:C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
. The same holds also for
\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
(up to changing the value of
C
).

If we choose
as above
M=\widetilde{M}
and
N\geq\widetilde{N}
,
not only we have
the bound
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)

but we also have the identity

\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x))=H(x,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x))

for
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}

(see also Remark
3.10
below), since
\lvert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(x,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x))\rvert\leq\widetilde{M}
for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]
. Thus,
in (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

), we can identify
\widetilde{H}
with
H
and hence assume it to be of class
\mathrm{C}^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

To estimate
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}
in
\mathrm{C}^{{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
independently of
N
, we
proceed by induction, proving iteratively bounds in

\mathrm{C}^{l}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for
l=1,\cdots,\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor
. The case
l=1
follows from the first step.
Next, we assume that there exist
l\in\{1,\cdots,\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor-1\}
and a natural number
\widetilde{N}=\widetilde{N}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},l)
such that, for every
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, it holds that
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}\in\mathrm{C}^{1,l}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
and

(3.12)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,l}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},l),

for a positive constant
C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},l)
.

As in the first step, our strategy relies on Duhamel’s representation formula. Back to (
3.9
),
the strategy is to take
l
derivatives in the right-hand side.
We notice that all the resulting derivatives of order
l
indeed exist thanks to the presence of the convolution by
D^{N}
, as a result of which we deduce that

\nabla^{l+1}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}
also exists and

(3.13)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,l+1}<\infty.

However, the bound clearly depends on
N
at this stage of the proof
(and this is the reason why one can prove that

\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}
belongs in fact to
\mathrm{C}^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, as claimed in the statement, but with a norm depending on
N
).

The objective is thus to get a bound
for (
3.13
)
that is independent of
N
, for
N
large enough
1
1

1

In fact, once the bound has been proved to be independent of

N
, for

N
large enough, one should be able to obtain a bound that is independent of
N
, for all
N
, just by taking the maximum with the bounds that hold true for smaller values of
N
.
We refrain from doing so because it would be useless for us.
.
To do this,
the key point is to provide a bound for

(3.14)

\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)\nabla^{l}\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s.

Proceeding as in the first step, we have

\displaystyle\lvert

\displaystyle\eqref{eqn: the_last_bad_guy}\rvert

(3.15)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)|\left|\nabla^{l}\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\right](y)-\nabla^{l}\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\right](y)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.16)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)|\left|\nabla^{l}\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\right](y)\right|\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s.

We recall that the map
x\mapsto\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))
is of class
\mathrm{C}^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with a norm uniformly bounded by a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
. Thus, the map
x\mapsto\nabla^{l}\left[\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla v^{\nu}(x))\right]
is of class
\mathrm{C}^{q-1-l}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with
q-1-l\geq q-1-(\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor-1)>d/2
. Then, by invoking the second statement of Lemma
A.3
, we obtain

(
3.16
)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{T}\frac{C(d,q)}{\sqrt{s-t}}\left\lVert\nabla^{l}\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\right]*D^{N}\right\rVert_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{T}\frac{C(d,q)}{\sqrt{s-t}}\left\lVert\nabla^{l}\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\right]\right\rVert_{2,q-1-l}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q),

where, to get the last constant, we used the inequality

\left\lVert\nabla^{l}\left[\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\right]\right\rVert_{2,q-1-l}\leq\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{\infty,q-1}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q).

For (
3.15
), we follow again the argument
used to treat
(
3.6
). Inserting directly the resulting bound into
(
3.14
), we get

(3.17)

\displaystyle\lvert\eqref{eqn: the_last_bad_guy}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t+N^{-2}}^{T}\lVert\nabla p_{s-t}\rVert_{2}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2,l}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+C(d)\int_{t}^{t+N^{-2}}\frac{N^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\sqrt{s-t}}\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2,l}\,\mathrm{d}s+C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q).

Third Step.
The difficulty now is to get an estimate for
\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2,l}
.
By using the bounds for the derivatives of
\widetilde{u}^{N,\nu}
up to the order
l
and the derivatives of
v^{\nu}
up to the order
l+1
, we obtain (the proof is given below in the fourth step)

(3.18)

\lVert\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\rVert_{2,l}\leq C(l)\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}-v^{\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2,l+1},

for a constant
C(l)
depending on
l
through the available
bounds for
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{u}_{t}^{N,\nu}\|_{\infty,l}
and

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v^{\nu}_{t}\|_{\infty,l+1}
, with the former being given by the induction
hypothesis (
3.12
).

We now turn to the estimates of the right-hand side in (
3.18
).
Following the proof of Lemma

3.6
,
we take
l+1
derivatives in the equations
(

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

) and (

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

)
and then make the difference between the two of them. Using the regularity of
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}
,
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}
, and
\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu})
, we have

(3.19)

\begin{split} &\|v_{t}^{\nu}-\tilde{u}_{t}^{N,\nu}\|^{2}_{2,l+1}+\int_{t}^{T}\|v_{s}^{\nu}-\tilde{u}_{s}^{N,\nu}\|^{2}_{2,l+2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},l)\left(\frac{1}{N^{2(q-1-l)}}+\int_{t}^{T}\|\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v_{s}^{\nu})*D^{N}-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu})*D^{N}\|^{2}_{2,l}\,\mathrm{d}s\right),\end{split}

where the exponent
q-1-l
comes from the fact that the
l+1
derivatives of

\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}
and
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}
in of the two equations
(

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

) and (

¯

⁢
H
J

ν

)
belong to
C^{q-1-l}
and the derivatives of
\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu})

up to the order
l
are also in
C^{q-1-l}
. Within this class, the rate of convergence
of the Dirichlet kernel is given by Lemma

A.5
and gives, at rank
N
, an error of order

N^{-(q-1-l)}
(and hence a squared error of order

N^{-2(q-1-l)}
).

Here, by Lemma
A.3
and (
3.18
),

\|\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v_{s}^{\nu})*D^{N}-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu})*D^{N}\|^{2}_{2,l}\leq\|\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v_{s}^{\nu})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})\|^{2}_{2,l}\leq C(l)\|\tilde{v}_{s}^{\nu}-\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\|^{2}_{2,l+1}.

By inserting the above into
(
3.19
) and then applying
Gronwall’s lemma, we get

\|v_{t}^{\nu}-\tilde{u}_{t}^{N,\nu}\|_{2,l+1}\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},l)}{N^{q-1-l}},

which makes it possible to come back to (
3.17
). By
using
the fact that
q-l-d/2\geq q-(q-2-d/2)-d/2=2
,
we get

\displaystyle\lvert\eqref{eqn: the_last_bad_guy}\rvert\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},l)\left(1+\frac{1}{N^{q-l-\frac{d}{2}}}\right)\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},l).

In (
3.9
), the remaining two terms of Duhamel’s representation of
\nabla^{l+1}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
can be handled as follows

\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}p_{T-t}(x-y)\left[\nabla^{l+1}[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}](\nu_{T},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\right\rvert\leq C(d,T,q)\left(\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}\rVert_{\infty,l+1}+\frac{{\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}\rVert_{\infty,q}}}{N^{q-1-l-\frac{d}{2}}}\right),

and

\left\lvert\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{s-t}(x-y)\left[\nabla^{l}[\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}](\nu_{s},\cdot)*D^{N}\right](y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rvert\leq C(d,T,q)\left(\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\infty,l}+\frac{{\lVert\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\infty,q}}}{N^{q-l-\frac{d}{2}}}\right).

Thus, combining the last three estimates
and using the bound
q-1-l-d/2\geq 1
, we obtain

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,l+1}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},l+1),

which the completes the induction.

Fourth Step.
It remains to prove (
3.18
). It suffices to study, for
r\in\{1,\dots,l\}
,

\bigl\|\nabla^{r}\bigl(\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\widetilde{H}(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}.

We focus on the worst case, that is
r=l
. As done e.g. in the proof of
[
16
, Lemma 5.4]
,
we notice that the difference above can be written as sum of terms of the form

\begin{split}&\partial_{x_{i}}^{l-k}\partial_{p_{i_{1}}\dots p_{i_{b}}}H(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})\partial_{x_{i}}^{j_{1}}\partial_{x_{i_{1}}}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\dots\partial_{x_{i}}^{j_{b}}\partial_{x_{i_{b}}}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\\
&\quad-\partial_{x_{i}}^{l-k}\partial_{p_{i_{1}}\dots p_{i_{b}}}H(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\partial_{x_{i}}^{j_{1}}\partial_{x_{i_{1}}}v^{\nu}_{s}\dots\partial_{x_{i}}^{j_{b}}\partial_{x_{i_{b}}}v^{\nu}_{s},\end{split}

with
k,b\in\{0,\dots,l\}
,
j_{1}+\dots+j_{b}=k
,

j_{1},\dots,j_{b}\geq 1
,

i,i_{1},\dots,i_{b}\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. When
b\geq 2
or
k\leq l-1
all the derivatives
of
v_{s}^{\nu}
appearing right above are of order less than
l-1+1=l
and are bounded by the induction hypothesis.
Therefore, we can

easily reformulate the above difference
of products as an increment of the form

\Psi\Bigl(\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s},\partial_{x_{i}}^{j_{1}},\partial_{x_{i_{1}}}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s},\dots,\partial_{x_{i}}^{j_{b}}\partial_{x_{i_{b}}}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\Bigr)-\Psi\Bigl(\nabla{u}^{N,\nu}_{s},\partial_{x_{i}}^{j_{1}},\partial_{x_{i_{1}}}{u}^{N,\nu}_{s},\dots,\partial_{x_{i}}^{j_{b}}\partial_{x_{i_{b}}}{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}\Bigr),

for a Lipschitz continuous function

\Psi
. It is then easy to bound the above difference by
the right-hand side in
(
3.18
).

When
b=1
, we have

\partial_{x_{i}}^{l-k}\partial_{p_{i_{1}}}H(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})\partial_{x_{i}}^{k}\partial_{x_{i_{1}}}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}-\partial_{x_{i}}^{l-k}\partial_{p_{i_{1}}}H(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\partial_{x_{i}}^{k}\partial_{x_{i_{1}}}v^{\nu}_{s},

and the conclusion is the same if
k\leq l-1
. In fact, the main difficulty arises when
b=1
and
k=l
.
To make the exposition clear, we feel better to switch back from partial derivatives to gradients and thus rewrite the above difference (in this case) in the form

\begin{split}&\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})\nabla^{l+1}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\nabla^{l+1}v^{\nu}_{s}\\
&=\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})\Bigl(\nabla^{l+1}\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s}-\nabla^{l+1}v^{\nu}_{s}\Bigr)+\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla v^{\nu}_{s})\Bigr)\nabla^{l+1}v^{\nu}_{s},\end{split}

which is enough to conclude. Indeed, to deal with the first term on the right-hand side we notice that
\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{s})
is bounded thanks to the first step and the regularity of
H
, while for the second one we combine the Lipschitz regularity of
p\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,p)
and the boundedness of
\nabla^{l+1}v^{\nu}
(see Proposition
3.2
)
∎

Remark 3.9
.

By Proposition
3.7
,
\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
is bounded (in
L^{\infty}
) by a constant that does not depend on
M
, at least when
N
is chosen larger than
\widetilde{N}
. This implies that the
L^{2}
bounds obtained in Proposition
3.5
are also independent of
M
(for the same range of values
for
N
).

Remark 3.10
.

Consistently with the argument used in the proof of
Proposition
3.7
, we stress that, under the conditions
M=\widetilde{M}

and
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, the auxiliary Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}
satisfies

\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x))=H(x,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x))
for any
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. In particular, under the same conditions, the two equations (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

) and (

~

⁢
H
J

N
,
ν

) coincide. More generally, it holds that
\widetilde{H}(x,p)=H(x,p)
whenever evaluated at
p=\nabla u^{\infty}(x)
,
\nabla v^{N,\nu}(x)
, and
\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}(x)
.

3.1.4.
Regularity of
u^{N,\nu}

We now return to the solution
u^{N,\nu}
of the original equation (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

).
As previously mentioned in Remark
3.10
, the crucial point is that, for
M=\widetilde{M}
and

N\geq\widetilde{N}
,

\widetilde{H}(x,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x))

coincides with
H(x,\nabla\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x))
. In this context,
\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}

solves (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

)
on the entire
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, and by uniqueness
of
the maximal solution to the latter equation (see Proposition
3.1
),

u^{N,\nu}

is also defined globally and coincides with

\tilde{u}^{N,\nu}
.
Consequently, the results obtained in Proposition
3.7
automatically transfer to
u^{N,\nu}
whenever
N\geq\widetilde{N}
. For convenience, let us restate these results in the context we are interested in.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be in force and let us consider
N\geq\widetilde{N}
.
Then,
u^{N,\nu}

is a global solution of (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

) and
belongs to
\mathrm{C}^{1,{q}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Moreover,

there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert u^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q).

A key feature of the bounds we have obtained is the fact that they are independent of the fixed flow
\nu\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, as well as of the index
N
(throughout, it is chosen greater than
\widetilde{N}
). This will play a key role in our fixed-point argument.

Remark 3.12
.

We have proved that for
N\geq\widetilde{N}
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\lVert u^{\infty}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,q}+\lVert v^{\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,q}+\lVert u^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}})\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
.
Moreover, we know from Assumption
2.2
- (H.5) that
H
belongs to
\mathrm{C}^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Therefore, the mappings
x\mapsto H(x,\nabla u_{t}^{\infty}(x))
,
x\mapsto H(x,\nabla v_{t}^{\nu}(x))
, and their derivatives up to order
{q-1}
, are bounded by a (uniform in time) constant which depends on
d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
,
H,q
.
The same holds for the mapping
x\mapsto H(x,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t}(x))
and its derivatives up to order
\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor
.
We recall that the estimates on
u^{N,\nu}_{t}
are not in the same class as those of
u^{\infty}_{t}
and
v^{\nu}_{t}
, see Remark
3.8
.

In the whole rest of the paper,
we will always consider
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, with
\widetilde{N}
as in Remark
3.10
. However, we will emphasize or recall this point when necessary.

3.2.
The approximating FP equation for a fixed flow in
\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

Let us fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, and consider again a flow
\nu\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. From Subsection
3.1
, there exists a unique classical solution
u^{N,\nu}
to (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

), which is bounded uniformly in time, and whose derivatives up to order
\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor
are also bounded.
We can then focus on the following version of the approximating Fokker-Planck equation (

⁢
F

P
N

):

(
FP_{N,\nu}
)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}&=\Delta\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}+\mathrm{div}\Big(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})(\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}*D^{N})\Big),\quad t\in(0,T],\\
\mu^{N,\nu}_{0}&=m.\end{cases}

Our main goal is to show that
\mu^{N,\nu}
is actually a probability measure, as well as
\mu^{N}*D^{N}
, at least when
N

is large enough. To this aim, we will take advantage of the results on
u^{N,\nu}
obtained in Subsection
3.1
, together with some further Sobolev estimates on
\mu^{N,\nu}
itself.

As initial step, we need to discuss the well-posedness of (

⁢
F

P

N
,
ν

). We proceed as we did for (

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

), by studying the Fourier coefficients of
\mu^{N,\nu}
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
fixed, it holds

(3.20)

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(k)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{0}(k)

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}(k).

The remaining coefficients
\{\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert>N}
can be obtained by solving a family of ODEs with a forcing term that depends upon the coefficients up to order
N
(i.e.,
\{\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(j)\}_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}
):

(3.21)

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(k)

\displaystyle=-\lvert 2\pi k\rvert^{2}\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(k)+g_{k}\left({u}^{N,\nu},\{\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(j)\}_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}\right),\quad\lvert k\rvert>N,

\displaystyle g_{k}\left(u^{N,\nu},\{\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(j)\}_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}2\pi k\cdot\sum_{\lvert k-l\rvert\leq N}\Big\{\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-32.76906pt\hbox{$\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla
