\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{0}(k)

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}(k).

The sequence of Fourier coefficients
\{\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}(k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is uniquely determined:

Proposition 3.13
.

Let Assumption
2.2
hold and
m\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Then the sequence of coefficients
\{\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}(k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is uniquely determined, and for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]
,
\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}(k)
is bounded by a constant which depends only on
d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,m,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
and
N
.
In particular, for any
N\geq\widetilde{N}
and
\nu\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, there exists a unique solution to
(

⁢
F

P

N
,
ν

) in
\mathrm{C}([0,T];L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
; when

m\in H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, the solution belongs to

\mathrm{C}^{1/2}([0,T];L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
.

Proof.

Thanks to Proposition
3.11
and the smoothness of
H
, the system (
3.20
)
(for
Fourier
modes

\lvert k\rvert\leq N
)
has Lipschitz coefficients and admits a unique solution. Once found the family
\{\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}(j)\}_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}
, the remaining coefficients can be easily computed by solving the linear ODEs (
3.21
).

To study the continuity in time of
\mu^{N,\nu}
, we decompose it in the form

\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}=\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}_{t}+\mu^{N,\nu,(2)}_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],

where
\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}
solves
the heat equation with
0
as initial condition and
\mathrm{div}\big(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})(\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}*D^{N})\big)
as a source term, i.e.,

(3.22)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}_{t}&=\Delta\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}_{t}+\mathrm{div}\Big(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})(\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}*D^{N})\Big),\quad t\in(0,T],\\
\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}_{0}&=0,\end{cases}

and
\mu^{N,\nu,(2)}
solves the standard heat equation with
m
as initial condition and
0
as source term.

Regarding
\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}
, we claim that the term
inside the divergence in (
3.22
)
belongs to
H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, uniformly in time.
This follows from the smoothness of
H
and the regularity of

u^{N,\nu}
established in
Proposition
3.11
(with
q\geq d+3
here), together with the smoothness of the convoluted function
\mu^{N,\nu}*D^{N}
. In turn, this
implies that
\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}
takes values in
H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, uniformly in time.

Integrating
(
3.22
)
with respect to

\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}_{t}-\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}_{s}

for a fixed time
s
, we deduce that

\mu^{N,\nu,(1)}
belongs to
\mathrm{C}^{1/2}([0,T];L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
.
Regarding
\mu^{N,\nu,(2)}
, the continuity in time is a standard consequence of the representation of the solution as a convolution with the heat kernel. Moreover, when
m\in H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

\mu^{N,\nu,(2)}
takes values in
H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, uniformly in time, and thus belongs to

\mathrm{C}^{1/2}([0,T];L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
.
∎

3.2.1.
An auxiliary FP equation

In this subsection we introduce the standard (hence simpler) version of (

⁢
F

P

N
,
ν

), which does not involve all the difficulties related to the convolution by the Dirichlet kernel
D^{N}
. It reads

(
\widetilde{FP}_{N,\nu}
)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}&=\Delta\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}+\mathrm{div}\big(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\big),\quad t\in(0,T],\\
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{0}&=m\in H^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).\end{cases}

We notice that (

~

⁢
F
P

N
,
ν

) is the classical FP equation associated with the drift
-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N}_{t}(\cdot))
and with diffusion of intensity
\sqrt{2}
. Thus, following for instance
[
4
, Chapter 6]
, we have that (

~

⁢
F
P

N
,
ν

) has a unique solution in
\mathrm{C}^{1/2}([0,T];\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
. Moreover,
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}
has a density in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for every
t\in[0,T]
. In the next proposition, we provide some further Sobolev estimates of
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let Assumption
2.2
hold and
m\in H^{{q-1}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Then,
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
is in the space
\mathrm{C}^{1/2,0}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
and for any
t\in[0,T]
,
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}
belongs to
H^{{q-1}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Moreover, there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2,\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}+\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\right)\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\lVert m\rVert_{2,\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.11
,
u^{N,\nu}_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert u^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
. From (

~

⁢
F
P

N
,
ν

) we have

\displaystyle\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\lVert m\rVert^{2}_{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lvert\langle\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{s})\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s},\nabla\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\lVert m\rVert^{2}_{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{s})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\left(\lVert m\rVert_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Thus, by Gronwall’s inequality,
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\lVert m\rVert_{2}
.

Let us proceed similarly for the gradient, and then sketch how to iterate this procedure to higher-order derivatives. By taking for any
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
the
i
-th partial derivative in space of (

~

⁢
F
P

N
,
ν

), we obtain

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}(\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t})&=\Delta(\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t})+\mathrm{div}\bigl(\partial_{x_{i}}\bigl[\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\bigr]\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\bigr)+\mathrm{div}(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}),\\
\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{0}&=\partial_{x_{i}}m,\end{cases}

where
\partial_{x_{i}}[\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})]=(\partial_{x_{i}}\mathrm{D}_{p}H)(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})+\mathrm{D}^{2}_{pp}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})(\partial_{x_{i}}\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})
, and
\partial_{x_{i}}
acts componentwise when applied to a vector-valued map.
Thus, by integrating against
\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\partial_{x_{i}}m\rVert_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mathrm{D}^{2}_{px}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla^{2}u^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\mathrm{D}^{2}_{pp}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{s})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\lVert\nabla\partial_{x_{i}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

By Proposition
3.11
,
u^{N,\nu}_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with
q\geq 2
and
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\lVert\nabla^{2}u^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)

(because
q-1-d/2\geq 2
). Moreover,
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}(\lVert\mathrm{D}^{2}_{px}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\mathrm{D}^{2}_{pp}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\rVert_{\infty})\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
because
H\in\mathrm{C}^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\nabla u^{N,\nu}
is uniformly bounded. Hence, we can conclude again by Young’s and then Gronwall’s inequalities.

So far, we have obtained a simple bound
for
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by using the fact that
u^{N,\nu}_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
H\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By the same technique, we can
bound

\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}
in
H^{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

by using the fact that
u^{N,\nu}
and
H
are of class
\mathrm{C}^{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}
.
The principle is as follows:
let us consider
2\leq l\leq\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor
and
assume by induction that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2,l-1}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\lVert m\rVert_{2,l-1}
.

For any
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{l})\in\{1,\dots,d\}^{l}
,
\partial^{l}_{x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{l}}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\partial^{l}_{x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{l}}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t})

\displaystyle=\Delta(\partial^{l}_{x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{l}}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t})+\mathrm{div}\Big\{\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\partial^{l}_{x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{l}}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{A\subset\{1,\dots,l\}:A\not=\emptyset}\mathrm{div}\Big\{\partial^{|A|}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{A}}}[\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})]\partial^{|A_{c}|}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{A_{c}}}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\Big\},

where the sum right above runs over non-empty subsets
A
of

\{1,\dots,l\}
. In the divergence,

|A|
denotes
the cardinal of
A
,
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{A}}
the tuple
(x_{i_{j}})_{j\in A}
and
A_{c}
the complementary of
A
(with similar notations for
|A_{c}|

and
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{A_{c}}}
).
Integrating the equation against

\partial^{l}_{x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{l}}}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}

and then
denoting by
\nabla^{l}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
the tensor of all
the
l
-derivatives of
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
,
we obtain (for a constant
C(l)
depending on
l
)

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla^{l}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla^{l+1}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\nabla^{l}m\rVert^{2}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla^{l+1}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C(l)}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\rVert^{2}_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla^{l}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C(l)}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\rVert^{2}_{\infty,l}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2,l-1}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla^{l+1}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

By recalling that
u^{N,\nu}_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
H\in\mathrm{C}^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
l+1\leq{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}
, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t})\rVert^{2}_{\infty,l}\leq C(\lVert H\rVert_{\infty,l+1},\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert u^{N,\nu}\rVert_{\infty,l+1}).

By Gronwall’s inequality and the induction hypothesis, we obtain, for every
l\leq\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2,l}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\lVert m\rVert_{2,l}.

Using the fact that
{u}^{N,\nu}
takes values in
\mathrm{C}^{q}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, but with bounds depending on
N
, we deduce in a similar manner that
the left-hand side is finite when
l=q-1
, but it may not be bounded uniformly in
N
.

It remains to check that
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}

is time-space continuous.
Following the proof of Proposition

3.13
, we can prove that

\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}\in\mathrm{C}^{1/2}([0,T];L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
.
Moreover,
since

\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}

takes values in
H^{{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, uniformly in time,
with
{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor=q-2+\lfloor-d/2\rfloor\geq d+1+\lfloor-d/2\rfloor}\geq d+1-d/2-1/2=d/2+1/2>d/2
, we deduce from Sobolev embedding that

\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
takes values in a compact subset of

\mathrm{C}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, uniformly in time. Joint time-space continuity follows from the combination of the last two
properties.
∎

We conclude our analysis of
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
by showing that, if the initial density is

strictly positive (uniformly on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
)
, then the same is true all along the flow of probability measures.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let Assumptions
2.2
and
2.10
be in force. Then, there exists a constant
\tilde{\gamma}=\tilde{\gamma}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q,\gamma)\in(0,1)
, such that
\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\geq\tilde{\gamma}>0
.

Proof.

From
[
3
, Theorem 1]
and
Proposition
3.11
, we have that the fundamental solution
\Gamma=\Gamma(x,t;y,\tau)
to the forward equation in (

~

⁢
F
P

N
,
ν

) is bounded from below by a positive constant
\bar{c}
times a suitable gaussian kernel
\bar{p}=\bar{p}_{t-\tau}(x-y)
, all depending only on
T
and the data. Thus, for any
0<\tau<t
and
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we have

\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Gamma(y,t;x,\tau)m(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\geq\gamma\bar{c}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bar{p}_{t-\tau}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y=:\tilde{\gamma}>0.

∎

3.2.2.
Proving that
\mu^{N,\nu}
and
\mu^{N,\nu}*D^{N}
are flow of probabilities

We now return to

\mu^{N,\nu}
.
As we have just done for
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
, we first prove that
\mu^{N,\nu}
has some Sobolev regularity when
m\in H^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let Assumption
2.2
hold, and
m\in H^{{q-1}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Then,
\mu^{N,\nu}
belongs to
\mathrm{C}^{1/2,0}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
and for any
t\in[0,T]
,
\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}
belongs to
H^{{q-1}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Moreover, there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\lVert\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2,{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}}+\lVert\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\right)\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\lVert m\rVert_{2,{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}}.

Proof.

Once noticed that
\lVert\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}*D^{N}\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}
(which follows from the contraction property of
D^{N}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
), the proof of the Sobolev regularity in space goes exactly as the one of Proposition
3.14
. Then, the
L^{\infty}
bound follows from Sobolev embedding. Continuity in time
can be also established as in the proof of
Proposition
3.14
.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

Note that in Propositions
3.14
and
3.16
, bounds are stated with respect to the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2,\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}
, even though the initial datum
m
is required to be more regular (it belongs to
H^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
). These (uniform in
N
) bounds cannot be improved in the sense that one cannot expect
better (uniform in
N
) regularity for

\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
and
{\mu}^{N,\nu}
(at least with the arguments used in the paper). The limitation comes from the fact that the (uniform in
N
) bounds
we have for
\nabla u^{N,\nu}
just hold true up to the order
\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor
. However, assumptions could be weakened: at this stage, we could just require
m
to belong to
H^{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. We would obtain the same results. The only difference is that in this case,
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}
and
\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}
would belong to
H^{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and not to
H^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(under the assumption
m\in H^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, they do belong to

H^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, but not uniformly in
N
). For simplicity, we prefer to keep
m\in H^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
as an assumption,
because this is the assumption we need in Section
4
.

We have all the ingredients to prove that, under suitable conditions,
\mu^{N,\nu}
is a probability measure when
N
is large. The result is obtained by comparing
\mu^{N,\nu}
with the probability
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let Assumptions
2.2
and
2.10
hold. Then, there exists a positive integer

\overline{N}=\overline{N}(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})

(independent of
\nu
and greater than the former choice of
\widetilde{N}
), such that for any
N\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{N}}
, the solution
\mu^{N,\nu}
of (

⁢
F

P

N
,
ν

) is a flow of probability measures. Moreover, there exists

\hat{\gamma}\in(0,1)
(independent of
\nu
) such that for any
N\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{N}}
,
\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}\geq\hat{\gamma}
.

Before proceeding with the proof of Proposition
3.18
, let us comment the notation for the threshold
\widetilde{N}
. As recalled above, we are always considering
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, with
\widetilde{N}
as in Remark
3.10
. Next, we also need
the conclusion of Proposition
3.18
to hold true and thus
N
to be also greater than
\overline{N}
(with this
\overline{N}

being in fact already assumed to be larger than
\widetilde{N}
). To remain consistent with our original notation, we will redefine
\widetilde{N}
as
\overline{N}

and then always consider
N\geq\widetilde{N}
in the sequel.

Proof.

From (
3.20
) it follows that
\widehat{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}(0)=1
for every
t\in[0,T]
, so that
\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}
has mass equal to
1
. To prove that
\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}
is positive, we show that
\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}-\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, where
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}
is the time-dependent probability measure that solves (

~

⁢
F
P

N
,
ν

). Indeed, with
p_{t}(x)
denoting the standard heat kernel on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}

\displaystyle(x)-\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{t-s}(x-y)\Big[\mathrm{D}_{p}H(y,\nabla u^{N}_{s}(y))\left(\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}(y)-\mu^{N,\nu}_{s}*D^{N}(y)\right)\Big]\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.23)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{t-s}(x-y)\mathrm{D}_{p}H(y,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{s}(y))\Big[\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}(y)-\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}*D^{N}(y)\Big]\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.24)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla p_{t-s}(x-y)\mathrm{D}_{p}H(y,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{s}(y))\Big[\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}*D^{N}(y)-\mu^{N,\nu}_{s}*D^{N}(y)\Big]\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s.

The fact that
\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\in H^{{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(with a norm bounded uniformly in time, see Proposition
3.14
), Lemma

A.6
and Proposition
3.11
entail

\displaystyle\lvert\eqref{eqn: estdiffmu2}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(d)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-s}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}-\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}*D^{N}\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{s})\rVert_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)}{N^{{\lfloor q-2-\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}-\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-s}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{2,{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)}{N^{{\lfloor q-2-\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}-\frac{d}{2}}}\lVert m\rVert_{2,q-1}.

Finally, from
Propositions

3.14

and

3.16

and
Remark
A.7
,

\displaystyle\lvert\eqref{eqn: estdiffmu3}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(d)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\sqrt{s-t}}\lVert\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{s})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert(\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}-\mu^{N,\nu}_{s})*D^{N}\rVert_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\left(\frac{\lVert m\rVert_{2,q-1}}{N^{\lfloor q-2-\frac{d}{2}\rfloor-\frac{d}{2}}}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-s}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{s}-\mu^{N,\nu}_{s}\rVert_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s\right),

and we can conclude by a generalized version of Gronwall’s inequality (that allows us to handle
1/{\sqrt{t-s}}
, see
[
33
]
for the result in full generality or
[
16
, Lemma A.1]
for the particular case used here) that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}-\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)}{N^{{\lfloor q-2-\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}-\frac{d}{2}}}\lVert m\rVert_{2,q-1}.

Since Lemma
3.15
implies
\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\tilde{\mu}^{N,\nu}_{t}\geq\tilde{\gamma}>0

and since

\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor-d/2\geq 1/2

(see the proof of Proposition
3.14
), we have that
\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}\geq\tilde{\gamma}/2:=\hat{\gamma}>0
for
N
sufficiently large.
∎

Remark 3.19
.

As a consequence of Propositions
3.16
and
3.18
, we can find
N
large enough (again we will say
N\geq\widetilde{N}
) and independent of
t\in[0,T]
, such that
not only
\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}
but
also
\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}*D^{N}
is a probability measure. Indeed, from Lemma
A.6
,

\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}*D^{N}(x)\geq\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}(x)-\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)}{N^{{\lfloor q-2-\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}-\frac{d}{2}}}\lVert\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2,{\lfloor q-2-\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}}\geq\hat{\gamma}-\frac{C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)}{N^{{\lfloor q-2-\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}-\frac{d}{2}}}\lVert m\rVert_{2,q-1}>0,

for
N
large enough. The fact that
\mu^{N,\nu}_{t}*D^{N}
is normalized trivially follows from the computation of the Fourier coefficient of order
0
.

3.3.
Well-posedness of the approximating forward-backward system

We finally go back to the original forward-backward approximating system (

⁢
H

J
N

) - (

⁢
F

P
N

) (or, equiv. (

⁢
F

B
N

)), that we re-state here to have a clearer exposition:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\mu^{N}_{t}&=\Delta\mu^{N}_{t}+\mathrm{div}(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N}_{t})(\mu^{N}_{t}*D^{N})),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\mu^{N}_{0}&=m,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\partial_{t}u^{N}_{t}&=-\Delta u^{N}_{t}+H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N}_{t})*D^{N}-\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{N}_{t}*D^{N},\cdot)*D^{N},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0ptu^{N}_{T}&=\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N},\cdot)*D^{N}.\end{cases}

The goal of this subsection is to combine the results of Subsections
3.1
and
3.2
with a suitable fixed point argument to conclude that there exists a classical solution
(\mu^{N},u^{N})
to (

⁢
F

B
N

), such that both
\mu^{N}
and
u^{N}
enjoy suitable regularity properties and
\mu^{N}
and
\mu^{N}*D^{N}
are flows of probabilities.

3.3.1.
A fixed-point argument and the existence of a solution to (

⁢
F

B
N

)

Let us stress again the fact that
N\geq\widetilde{N}

(recall
Remark

3.19
and Proposition
3.18
). We define the space
X\subseteq\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))

of time-dependent measures
\nu
with null Fourier coefficients of order higher than
N
such that, for a certain
C>0

(possibly depending on
N
)
and any
s,t\in[0,T]
,

(3.25)

\lVert\nu_{t}-\nu_{s}\rVert_{2}=\Bigg(\sum_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}\lvert\hat{\nu}_{t}(k)-\hat{\nu}_{s}(k)\rvert^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\lvert t-s\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We equip
\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with the topology induced by the
L^{2}
convergence
(which is in fact the same as the topology induced by any standard norm since

\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is finite-dimensional), and so by Ascoli-Arzelà theorem it follows that
X
is compact with respect to the uniform norm in time. Moreover an easy calculation shows that
X
is also convex. Our aim is to build a suitable fixed point argument for a mapping over
X
.

Proposition 3.20
.

Let Assumptions
2.2
and
2.10
be in force. For
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, there exists a unique solution
(\mu^{N},u^{N})
to the forward-backward system (

⁢
F

B
N

). More precisely,
u^{N}
is a classical solution and belongs to
\mathrm{C}^{1,{q}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
;
\mu^{N}
is in
\mathrm{C}^{1/2,0}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
and admits a density in
H^{q-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Moreover,
there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\mu^{N}_{t}\rVert_{2,{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q)\lVert m\rVert_{2,q-1},

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert u^{N}_{t}\rVert_{\infty,{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}}\leq C(d,T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},H,q).

Finally,
\mu^{N}*D^{N}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
.

Proof.

Uniqueness is addressed separately in the forthcoming Subsection
3.3.2
. We focus on the existence. Let us fix
\nu\in X
. Then, by Proposition
3.18
and Remark
3.19
we have that
\mu^{N,\nu}
and
\mu^{N,\nu}*D^{N}
belong to
\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
and
\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
respectively. Using the form of (

⁢
F

P

N
,
ν

) (reformulated in Fourier coefficients)
and Proposition

3.11
, we see
that (
3.25
) holds for
\mu^{N,\nu}*D^{N}
for a certain constant
C
independent of
\nu
.
Thus, for every
N\geq\widetilde{N}
, we can define the mapping
\Phi\colon X\to X
by
\nu\mapsto\Phi(\nu):=\mu^{N,\nu}*D^{N}
.

We now prove that
\Phi
is continuous. Let us consider a sequence
\{\nu^{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\subset X
such that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\nu^{\ell}_{t}-\nu_{t}\rVert_{2}\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
, for a
\nu\in X
.
The proof relies on the ODE interpretation of
(

⁢
H

J

N
,
ν

)–(

⁢
F

P
N

).
First, we claim that
\lVert u^{N,\nu^{\ell}}_{t}-u^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
and hence
\lVert\nabla u^{N,\nu^{\ell}}_{t}-\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{t}\rVert_{2}\to 0
thanks to the relation
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-7.86227pt\hbox{$\nabla u$}\hskip-7.86227pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(k)=\mathrm{i}2\pi k\widehat{u}(k)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Indeed, the Lipschitz property in the measure argument (with respect to the
L^{2}
norm and uniformly with respect to
x
) of
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}
implies, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
\lvert\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-19.22751pt\hbox{$\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu^{\ell}_{t},\cdot)$}\hskip-19.22751pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{19.49014pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(k)-\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-19.0643pt\hbox{$\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\nu_{t},\cdot)$}\hskip-19.0643pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{18.50012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(k)\rvert\leq C(d,\mathcal{F})\lVert\nu_{t}^{\ell}-\nu_{t}\rVert_{2}
, and similarly for
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}
. Recalling that
\{\widehat{u}^{N,\nu}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert>N}
is equal to
0
and that
\{\widehat{u}^{N,\nu}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}
solves (
3.1
), continuity (in
\nu
) of
u^{N,\nu}
is a consequence of the Cauchy-Lipschitz
stability theory for
ODEs
(see the introduction of §
3.1.4
).

With this, we can argue in the same way from (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) and prove that
\lVert\mu^{N,\nu^{\ell}}-\mu^{N,\nu}\rVert_{2}\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
.

Since we proved that
\Phi\colon X\to X
is continuous and defined over a compact and convex set, we can apply the Shauder fixed-point theorem and obtain that
\Phi
has a fixed-point. This proves the existence of a flow of probabilities
\mu^{N}*D^{N}
(i.e., determined by
\{\hat{\mu}^{N}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}
) solving
the forward equation in
(

⁢
F

B
N

), when written in the form
(
3.20
), as well as the existence of a solution
u^{N}
to the backward equation, when written in the form
(
3.1
). The flow of probabilities
\mu^{N}

(i.e.,
\{\hat{\mu}^{N}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert>N}
)
can be easily determined by (
3.21
).
Finally the estimates in
\mathrm{C}^{{\lfloor q-1-d/2\rfloor}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
H^{{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
norms follow from Propositions
3.11
and
3.16
.
∎

3.3.2.
Stability and uniqueness

Here we provide a stability result for the forward-backward system (

⁢
F

B
N

), which implies the uniqueness property claimed in Proposition
3.20
. In order to have lighter and clearer notation, we omit the superscript
N
when we refer to a pair
(\mu^{N},u^{N})
that solves the system (

⁢
F

B
N

) (but this is only in this paragraph that we do so). The proof
relies on the convexity properties of
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
and
follows the classical duality argument from the mean field game literature
on monotone forward-backward systems
(see, e.g.
[
8
, Lemma 3.1.2]
).

Proposition 3.21
.

Let Assumption
2.2
hold and
N\geq\widetilde{N}
. Let
\{m^{i}\}_{i=1,2}
be two probability measures satisfying Assumption
2.10
, and
\{(\mu^{i},u^{i})\}_{i=1,2}
be two solutions of the forward-backward system (

⁢
F

B
N

) starting respectively from
\{m^{i}\}_{i=1,2}
. Then,
there exists a constant
C=C(d,T,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}},H)
such that

\int_{0}^{T}\langle\rvert\nabla u^{2}_{t}-\nabla u^{1}_{t}\lvert^{2},\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N}+\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\langle m^{1}-m^{2},u^{1}_{0}-u^{2}_{0}\rangle.

In particular, the forward-backward system (

⁢
F

B
N

) has a unique solution.

Proof.

Let us consider the two equations satisfied by
(u^{1}-u^{2})
and
(\mu^{1}-\mu^{2})
, obtained by subtracting the two versions of (

⁢
F

B
N

) with initial conditions
\{m^{i}\}_{i=1,2}
. Integration by parts leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\langle\mu^{1}_{t}-\mu^{2}_{t},u^{1}_{t}-u^{2}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{1}_{t})(\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N})-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{2}_{t})(\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N}),\nabla u^{1}_{t}-\nabla u^{2}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\langle\mu^{1}_{t}-\mu^{2}_{t},H(\cdot,\nabla u^{1}_{t})*D^{N}-H(\cdot,\nabla u^{2}_{t})*D^{N}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\langle\mu^{1}_{t}-\mu^{2}_{t},\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N},\cdot)*D^{N}-\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N},\cdot)*D^{N}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\{H(\cdot,\nabla u^{2}_{t})-H(\cdot,\nabla u^{1}_{t})-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{1}_{t})\cdot(\nabla u^{2}_{t}-\nabla u^{1}_{t})\},\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\langle\{H(\cdot,\nabla u^{1}_{t})-H(\cdot,\nabla u^{2}_{t})-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{2}_{t})\cdot(\nabla u^{1}_{t}-\nabla u^{2}_{t})\},\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\langle\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N}-\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N},\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N},\cdot)-\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N},\cdot)\rangle,

where we used the symmetry of the Dirichlet kernel to move
D^{N}
from one side to the other in the duality brackets. Then, by integrating in time and using the monotonicity of
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}
and
\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\{H(\cdot,\nabla u^{2}_{t})

\displaystyle-H(\cdot,\nabla u^{1}_{t})-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{1}_{t})\cdot(\nabla u^{2}_{t}-\nabla u^{1}_{t})\},\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\{H(\cdot,\nabla u^{1}_{t})-H(\cdot,\nabla u^{2}_{t})-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{2}_{t})\cdot(\nabla u^{1}_{t}-\nabla u^{2}_{t})\},\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\langle m^{1}-m^{2},u^{1}_{0}-u^{2}_{0}\rangle.

The strong convexity of
H
implies, for a positive
C
, the desired stability property:

\int_{0}^{T}\langle\rvert\nabla u^{2}_{t}-\nabla u^{1}_{t}\lvert^{2},\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N}+\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\langle m^{1}-m^{2},u^{1}_{0}-u^{2}_{0}\rangle.

In particular, if we consider
m^{1}=m^{2}
, the right-hand side in the above estimate vanishes. Since
\mu^{1}_{t}*D^{N}
and
\mu^{2}_{t}*D^{N}
are measures with positive densities
(see Proposition
3.18
), we have that
\nabla u^{1}=\nabla u^{2}
in
(0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. We can then realize that
\mu^{1}=\mu^{2}
, since they solve the same Fokker-Planck equation. In the same manner we conclude that
u^{1}=u^{2}
, and so we have obtained uniqueness for the forward-backward system (

⁢
F

B
N

).
∎

3.3.3.
A nonlinear system of ODEs for the Fourier coefficients of
(\mu^{N},u^{N})

The purpose of this final paragraph on the approximating forward-backward system is to collect the properties of the Fourier coefficients of its unique solution
(\mu^{N},u^{N})
. The results we present here are merely a restatement of the results we obtained earlier in the initial part of Subsections
3.1
and
3.2
, with the additional insight provided by the fixed-point argument and the existence result established in Proposition
3.20
.

First, let us recall that due to the presence of the Dirichlet kernel, the solution to (

⁢
H

J
N

) has null Fourier coefficients of order greater than
N
. On the other hand, the coefficients
\{\widehat{u}^{N}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}
can be computed by solving the system of nonlinear ODEs

) depends only upon the coefficients up to order

.

Looking more carefully at (
3.26
), we can observe that only
\mu^{N}*D^{N}
is involved in the equation. This means that we only need the Fourier coefficients
\{\widehat{\mu}^{N}(j)\}_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}

). Moreover, the nonlinear term
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-23.71713pt\hbox{$H(\cdot,\nabla 451.29065pt}(k)
depends on
\nabla u^{N}
, which can be expressed in terms of
\{\widehat{u}^{N}(j)\}_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}
. The same holds for the term involving
\nabla u^{N}
in (
3.27
). Thus, we can join (
3.26
) and (
3.27
) to obtain a nonlinear system of ODEs in closed form which involves only the finite sequences of Fourier coefficients
\{\widehat{\mu}^{N}(j)\}_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}
and
\{\widehat{u}^{N}(j)\}_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}
. We collect this observation, as well as the previous comment, in the following theorem:

Theorem 3.22
.

Let Assumptions
2.2
and
2.10
hold, and let
N\geq\widetilde{N}
. Then, the Fourier coefficients
\{(\hat{\mu}^{N}(k),\hat{u}^{N}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}
of the unique solution
(\mu^{N},u^{N})
to (

⁢
F

B
N

) are uniquely determined by the nonlinear system of ODEs (
3.26
)–(
3.27
), which is in closed form.
Moreover,
\{\hat{u}^{N}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert>N}
are identically null, whilst
\{\hat{\mu}^{N}(k)\}_{\lvert k\rvert>N}
solve the linear system (
3.28
).

The result stated in Theorem
3.22
is one of the most important feature of the approximating forward-backward system (

⁢
F

B
N

). Indeed, it provides an explicit rule to compute the Fourier coefficients of order
\lvert k\rvert\leq N
by solving a finite dimensional system of ODEs. This, combined with the convergence results obtained in the forthcoming Section
4
, gives a way to reduce the infinite dimensional MFC control problem to a finite dimensional one.

Remark 3.23
.

Throughout our discussion on the forward-backward system (

⁢
F

B
N

), and particularly for the approximated Fokker-Planck equation (

⁢
F

P
N

), we have considered an initial condition
m
that satisfies Assumption
2.10
and may have an infinite number of non-zero Fourier coefficients. However, in the spirit of the comment above, we can also consider an initial condition with a finite number of non-zero Fourier coefficients (for instance, with coefficients of order greater than
N
being null). With this in mind, one can consider the original MFC problem and the system (

⁢
F

B
∞

) starting from a general
m
satisfying Assumption
2.10
, and then look at the approximating system starting from
m*D^{N}
(which remains a strictly positive density as long as we take
N
sufficiently large).

Example 3.24
.

Let us consider the case of a quadratic Hamiltonian, that is
H(x,p)=\frac{1}{2}\lvert p\rvert^{2}
. Since
\lvert p\rvert^{2}=p\cdot p
, we can use the discrete convolution to compute
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-23.71713pt\hbox{$H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N}_{t})$}\hskip-23.71713pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{19.41238pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(k)
.
Then, the ODE in (
3.26
) reads, for any
\lvert k\rvert\leq N
,

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\widehat{u}^{N}_{t}(k)=\lvert 2\pi k\rvert^{2}\widehat{u}^{N}_{t}(k)-\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-33.321pt\hbox{$\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{N}_{t}*D^{N},\cdot)$}\hskip-33.321pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{19.41238pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(k)-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\pi^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert k-l\rvert\leq N\\
\lvert l\rvert\leq N\end{subarray}}[(k-l)\widehat{u}^{N}_{t}(k-l)]\cdot[l\ \widehat{u}^{N}_{t}(l)].

3.4.
Characterization of the optimal control for the approximating MFC problem

We have all the tools to rigorously prove that the pair
(\mu^{N},u^{N})
, which is the unique solution to (

⁢
F

B
N

), is optimal for the approximating control problem (
2.5
) (with
t=0
). More precisely, the optimal feedback control is unique and given by
\alpha^{*,N}=-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N})
, with
\mu^{N}
as
optimal trajectory. Moreover, we can also introduce an equivalent formulation of the approximating MFC problem as a finite-dimensional control problem.

3.4.1.
Existence and uniqueness of the optimal control

Intuitively, the system (

⁢
F

B
N

)
should be regarded as the Pontryagin system
associated with the approximating MFC problem (
2.5
).
The following result clarifies this fact:
using convexity of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, we show that
\alpha^{*,N}=-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N})
is indeed the unique minimizer of (
2.5
). The proof follows the one of
[
8
, Proposition 3.7.2]
.

Proposition 3.25
.

Let Assumptions
2.2
and
2.10
hold, and let
N\geq\widetilde{N}
. Then, the control
\alpha^{*,N}=-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N})
is the unique minimizer of the approximating MFC problem (
2.5
), where
(\mu^{N},u^{N})
is the unique solution to (

⁢
F

B
N

).

Proof.

Let
\check{\alpha}^{N}
be a feedback control for (
2.5
) such that the associated trajectory
\check{\mu}^{N}*D^{N}
is a flow of probability measures. Let us recall that
L(x,\alpha)=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(-\alpha\cdot p-H(x,p)\right)

is uniformly strictly convex in
\alpha
thanks to the uniform strict convexity of
H
in the momentum
p
.
Therefore, there exists a constant
c>0
such that, for any
(x,\alpha)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\begin{split}L(x,\alpha)&\geq L(x,\alpha^{*,N}(t,x))+\partial_{\alpha}L(x,\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x))\cdot(\alpha-\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x))+c\bigl|\alpha-\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x)\bigr|^{2}\\
&=-\nabla u^{N}_{t}(x)\cdot\alpha+L(x,\alpha^{*,N}(t,x))+\nabla u^{N}_{t}(x)\cdot\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x)+c\bigl|\alpha-\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x)\bigr|^{2}\\
&=-\nabla u^{N}_{t}(x)\cdot\alpha-H(x,\nabla u^{N}_{t}(x))+c\bigl|\alpha-\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x)\bigr|^{2},\end{split}

where we used the following two identities:

\partial_{\alpha}L(x,\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x))=-\nabla u^{N}_{t}(x),\quad-\alpha_{t}^{*,N}(x)\cdot\nabla u^{N}_{t}(x)-H(x,\nabla u^{N}_{t}(x))=L(x,\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x)).

Then, combining this with the convexity of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, we get

\displaystyle J^{N}(\check{\alpha}^{N},0,m)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Big[-\check{\alpha}^{N}_{s}(x)\cdot\nabla u^{N}_{s}(x)-H(x,\nabla u^{N}_{s}(x))\Big](\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N})(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\{\mathcal{F}(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N})+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N},x)\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N}-\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\bigg\}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{G}(\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N})+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N},x)\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{T}*D^{N}-\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\quad+c\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl|\check{\alpha}^{N}_{t}(x)-\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x)\bigr|^{2}(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N})(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Using the identity
-\alpha^{*,N}\cdot\nabla u^{N}-H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N})=L(\cdot,\alpha^{*,N})
, it follows

\displaystyle J^{N}(\check{\alpha}^{N},0,m)

\displaystyle\geq J^{N}(\alpha^{*,N},0,m)+c\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl|\check{\alpha}^{N}(x)-\alpha^{*,N}_{t}(x)\bigr|^{2}(\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N})(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.29)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u^{N}_{s}(x)\cdot\Big[\check{\alpha}^{N}(x)(\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N})-\alpha^{*,N}(x)(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N})\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.30)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}H(x,\nabla u^{N}_{s}(x))\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N}-\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.31)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N},x)\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N}-\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.32)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N},x)\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{T}*D^{N}-\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x).

To conclude, let us prove that
\eqref{eqn: opt_1}+\eqref{eqn: opt_2}+\eqref{eqn: opt_3}+\eqref{eqn: opt_4}=0
. By using the fact that
\mu^{N}
and
\check{\mu}^{N}
solve (
2.4
), together with integration by parts, we get

(
3.29
)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{N}_{s}(x)\mathrm{div}\Big\{\check{\alpha}^{N}(x)\big(\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N}(x)\big)-\alpha^{*,N}(x)\big(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N}(x)\big)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{N}_{s}(x)\big(\mu^{N}_{s}(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)-\check{\mu}_{s}^{N}(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\big)\bigg]_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Big(\partial_{s}u^{N}_{s}(x)+\Delta u^{N}_{s}(x)\Big)\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}-\mu^{N}_{s}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Then, since
u^{N}
solves (

⁢
H

J
N

) and by the symmetry of the Dirichlet kernel
D^{N}
, we obtain

(
3.29
)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{G}(\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N},x)\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{T}*D^{N}-\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{F}(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N},x)\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N}-\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}H(x,\nabla u^{N}_{s}(x))\Big[\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N}-\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N}\Big](\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s,

which proves that

\eqref{eqn: opt_1}+\eqref{eqn: opt_2}+\eqref{eqn: opt_3}+\eqref{eqn: opt_4}
is indeed equal to
0
and therefore that

\alpha^{*,N}
is optimal. Furthermore, if
\check{\alpha}^{N}
is also optimal, then

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl|\check{\alpha}^{N}_{s}(x)-\alpha^{*,N}_{s}(x)\bigr|^{2}(\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N})(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s=0.

Recalling that, for each
s\in[0,T]
,
\check{\mu}^{N}_{s}*D^{N}
is assumed to be a probability density, we notice that it must be strictly positive
except maybe at a finite number of zeros (as a non-trivial trigonometric polynomial function has at most a finite number of zeros). Therefore, we get
that, for almost every
s\in[0,T]
,
\check{\alpha}^{N}_{s}
and

\alpha^{*,N}_{s}*D^{N}
are almost everywhere equal. This completes the proof.
∎

3.4.2.
The approximating MFC problem as a finite dimensional control problem

Here we continue the discussion started in Subsection
3.3.3
about the Fourier expansion of the optimal pair
(\mu^{N},u^{N})
. Let us go back to the original formulation of our approximating MFC problem stated in Subsection
2.1.2
, with
t=0
.
For an initial condition

m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that

m*D^{N}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, the cost functional to be minimized is

(3.33)

J^{N}(\alpha,0,m)=\mathcal{G}(\mu^{N}_{T}*D^{N})+\int_{0}^{T}\left\{\mathcal{F}(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N})+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}L(x,\alpha_{s}(x))(\mu^{N}_{s}*D^{N})(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\right\}\,\mathrm{d}s,

over the set of bounded and Borel measurable feedback controls
\alpha:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
for which the flow

(\mu^{N}_{t}*D^{N})_{0\leq t\leq T}
takes values in

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

(or equivalently in

{\mathcal{P}}_{N}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
).
From Subsection
3.4.1
, we know that the optimal control must be
\alpha^{*,N}(\cdot)=-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N}(\cdot))
, where
u^{N}
solves (

⁢
H

J
N

) and has null Fourier coefficients of order
\lvert k\rvert>N
(see Theorem
3.22
). Moreover, from (
3.33
) it is clear that,
for an arbitrary control
\alpha
,
only the first
\lvert k\rvert\leq N
Fourier coefficients of the state variable
\mu^{N}
are involved in the optimization problem, and they can be computed through the analogue of the closed system of linear ODEs (
3.27
)
(with
the Fourier coefficients

\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-29.84775pt\hbox{$\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla u^{N})$}\hskip-29.84775pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{19.41238pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(k-l)
being replaced by
-\widehat{\alpha}(k-l)
). In particular, we have
J^{N}(\alpha,0,m)=J^{N}(\alpha,0,m*D^{N})
.

Thus, it becomes natural to introduce the following finite-dimensional control problem. Let us introduce the set
{\mathscr{O}}_{N}
of vectors of complex numbers
\mathbf{z}=\{z_{k}\}_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}

(with
z_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}
for each
k
)
which are Fourier coefficients of a probability measure in
\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(a precise definition and a characterization of this set can be found in
[
13
, Section 3.1]
). For
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathscr{O}}_{N}
, we consider the system of controlled ODEs

(3.34)

\begin{cases}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\xi^{N}_{t}(k)&=-\lvert 2\pi k\rvert^{2}\xi^{N}_{t}(k)-\textrm{\rm i}2\pi k\cdot\sum_{\lvert k-l\rvert\leq N}\widehat{\alpha}_{t}(l)\xi^{N}_{t}(k-l),\quad\lvert k\rvert\leq N,\\
\quad\\
\xi^{N}_{0}(k)&=z_{k},\end{cases}

where
\{(\xi_{t}(k))_{0\leq t\leq T}\}_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}
are
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions, and we associate with it the cost functional

\displaystyle\Gamma^{N}(\alpha,0,\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}\Big(\sum_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}\xi^{N}_{T}(k)e_{k}\Big)+\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\{\mathcal{F}\Big(\sum_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}\xi^{N}_{s}(k)e_{k}\Big)+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}L\big(x,\alpha_{s}(x)\big)\Big(\sum_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}\xi^{N}_{s}(k)e_{k}\Big)(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\bigg\}\,\mathrm{d}s.

We claim that the infimum of

\Gamma^{N}(\alpha,0,\mathbf{z})
over the class of bounded and measurable feedback controls
\alpha
for which
(\{\xi^{N}_{t}(k)\}_{|k|\leq N})_{0\leq t\leq T}
takes values in
{\mathscr{O}}_{N}

is equal to

V^{N}(t,m)
in (
2.5
),
where
m:=\sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
The proof is as follows. We can extend the collection
\{(\xi_{t}(k))_{0\leq t\leq T}\}_{|k|\leq N}
to indices

k
such that
|k|>N
, by letting

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\xi^{N}_{t}(k)=-\lvert 2\pi k\rvert^{2}\xi^{N}_{t}(k)-\textrm{\rm i}2\pi k\cdot\sum_{\lvert k-l\rvert\leq N}\widehat{\alpha}_{t}(l)\xi^{N}_{t}(k-l),\quad\lvert k\rvert>N.

It is pretty obvious to see that
\overline{\xi_{t}^{N}(k)}=\xi_{t}^{N}(-k)
(with

\bar{z}
denoting the conjugate of
z
, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
), from which we deduce that, for each
t\in[0,T]
,

\check{\mu}^{N}_{t}:=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\xi_{t}^{N}(k)e_{k}

is a real-valued Schwartz distribution (this would be possible to find an index
{\mathfrak{s}}>0
, depending on the dimension

d
, such that
\check{\mu}^{N}_{t}\in H^{2,-{\mathfrak{s}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
for any
t\in[0,T]
, but there is no need for us to push the analysis further in this direction).
In this manner, we can see

\{\xi^{N}_{t}(k)\}_{|k|\leq N}
as the lower Fourier coefficients of

\check{\mu}^{N}_{t}

and hence identify
\Gamma^{N}(\alpha,0,{\mathbf{z}})

with
J^{N}(\alpha,0,m)
.
In particular, the minimum of
\Gamma^{N}(\alpha,0,\mathbf{z})

is attained at

(\alpha_{t}^{*,N}=-\mathrm{D}_{p}H(\cdot,\textrm{\rm i}2\pi\sum_{\lvert l\rvert\leq N}l\widehat{u}^{N}_{t}(l)e_{l}))_{0\leq t\leq T}

and the latter is the unique minimizer,
with
\{(\widehat{u}^{N}_{t}(l))_{0\leq t\leq T}\}_{|l|\leq N}
solving
(
3.34
).
The optimal trajectory is thus given by

\{(\xi^{N}_{t}(k))_{0\leq t\leq T}\}_{|k|\leq N}=\{(\widehat{\mu}^{N}_{t}(k))_{0\leq t\leq T}\}_{|k|\leq N}
, see (
3.27
).

To sum-up, if we denote by
\Gamma^{N}\left(\alpha,t,\textbf{z}\right)
the cost functional for the finite-dimensional problem starting at
t\in[0,T]
instead of
0
and we introduce the value function

(3.35)

\Upsilon^{N}(t,\mathbf{z}):=\inf\Gamma^{N}\left(\alpha,t,\textbf{z}\right),\quad(t,\mathbf{z})\in[0,T]\times{\mathscr{O}}_{N},

with the infimum being taken over the same feedback functions
\alpha
as before (except that

\alpha
is defined on
[t,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
),
we have
V^{N}(t,m)=V^{N}(t,m*D^{N})=\Upsilon^{N}(t,\{z_{k}\}_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N})

for
m=\sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
Remarkably, the state variable
in the controlled dynamics
(
3.34
)
is finite-dimensional.
Also, given the shape of the optimal feedback function
\alpha^{*,N}
, we can restrict the choice of
the controls
\alpha
(which are
a priori
infinite-dimensional) to the
subclass of time measurable functions taking values in the
parametric (hence finite-dimensional) set

{\mathscr{A}}_{N}:=\Bigl\{a=-{\rm D}_{p}H\Bigl(\cdot,\textrm{\rm i}2\pi\sum_{|l|\leq N}l\zeta(l)e_{l}\Bigr),\ \ \textrm{\rm with}\ \ \zeta(l)\in{\mathbb{C}}:\overline{\zeta(l)}=\zeta(-l),\ \ |l|\leq N\Bigr\}.

We then call
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}

the set of measurable
functions from
[t,T]
to
{\mathscr{A}}_{N}

(for simplicity, the index
t
is omitted in the notation
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
).

The following lemma provides a sufficient (and explicit) condition (on
\alpha
) under which
the solution to
(
3.34
) takes values in

{\mathscr{O}}_{N}
:

Lemma 3.26
.

For fixed

constants
c,C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
consider
\alpha=(\alpha_{s})_{t\leq s\leq T}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
such that
the weights
\{(\zeta_{s}(l))_{t\leq s\leq T}\}_{|l|\leq N}

in the parametric representation of
\alpha

satisfy

(3.36)

\sum_{|l|\leq N} |l|^{{2d+6}}|\zeta_{s}(l)|^{2}\leq C,\quad s\in[t,T].

Then,
there exists
an integer
\widetilde{N}
, depending on
c
,
C
and
\gamma
, such that
for
N\geq\widetilde{N}

and

m:=\sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

satisfying

m\geq\gamma

and
\sum_{|k|\leq N}|z_{k}|^{2}(1+|k|^{2})^{q-1}\leq c
,
the solution to
(
3.34
)
(starting at time
t
)
takes values in

{\mathscr{O}}_{N}
.

Proof.

We let

v_{s}:=\sum_{|l|\leq N{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\,l\not=0}} \zeta_{s}(l)e_{l},\quad s\in[t,T].

By assumption,
it is bounded in

H^{d+3}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
independently of
N
.
By Sobolev embedding,

\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\|v_{s}\|_{{\infty,r}}\leq\widetilde{C},

for some
r\geq\lfloor d/2+2\rfloor
and some
\widetilde{C}
depending on
C
.
The result then follows from the same analysis as the one developed in
Proposition

3.18
and
Remark
3.19

(in short,
\lfloor d/2+2\rfloor
corresponds to
the regularity order of
u^{N,\nu}
in the latter two statements when
q
is equal to the minimal threshold
d+3
; here, the role of

\nabla u^{N,\nu}_{s}
is played by
\nabla v_{s}
and, in fact, the zero-order Fourier mode of
v_{s}
does not matter
).
∎

Of course, Proposition

3.20

says that the
optimal feedback

\alpha^{*,N}

satisfies
(
3.36
) when
q\geq 3d/2+4

and
C
is sufficiently large (with respect to
the value of

\sup_{t\leq s\leq T}\|u^{N}_{s}\|_{q,\infty}
).
In this way, we have characterized
the optimal feedback
\alpha^{*,N}
as the
solution of
the control problem
(
3.35
) defined
over
controls in
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
satisfying
(
3.36
)
: both the state-space
{\mathscr{O}}_{N}

and the control-space
{\mathscr{A}}_{N}
(with the corresponding version of the condition
(
3.36
))
are finite dimensional.

3.4.3.
The HJB equation associated to the approximating MFC problem

To conclude this section, we discuss (at least at a formal level) the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation associated

with
the approximating MFC problem (
2.5
) and the equivalent finite-dimensional optimal control problem (
3.35
). First, following the usual steps based on the dynamic programming principle, we obtain the following HJB equation
for (
2.5
):

(3.37)

\begin{cases}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\partial_{t}V^{N}(t,m)+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Delta[\delta_{\mu}V^{N}](t,m,x)m(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)\\
\quad\quad-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(H(\cdot,\nabla[\delta_{\mu}V^{N}](t,m,\cdot))*D^{N}\right)(x)m(\!\,\mathrm{d}x)+\mathcal{F}(m*D^{N})=0,\\
\quad\\
V^{N}(T,m)=\mathcal{G}(m*D^{N}),\end{cases}

on a suitable subset

\mathcal{B}
of
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Although
the form of
(
3.37
)
is
fairly predictible

(because it is very similar to the HJB equation for the original MFC problem),
the choice of the domain
\mathcal{B}
raises interesting questions.

In line
with the
statement
of
Proposition
3.18
,
one should restrict
(
3.37
)
to pairs
(t,m)

for which
m*D^{N}
is
bounded from below by a strictly positive constant
and has a Sobolev norm bounded by another fixed constant
.
The difficulty
arises
from the fact that the lower bound
(which is a constraint on the initial condition
(t,m)
)
may not be preserved by the Fokker-Planck dynamics
(
2.4
), even within the setting of
Proposition
3.18
.

While
this has a limited (mostly technical) impact
on
the formal derivation of the HJB equation (the proof usually
consists of
a verification argument based upon
the dynamic programming principle), this raises more challenging questions about the well-posedness

of
the above equation,

because

the restriction of the domain to the smaller subset

{\mathcal{B}}
of
[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}^{d}})
creates a boundary.

To overcome this drawback and free ourselves from the influence of the boundary, we would ideally have to choose a set
{\mathcal{B}}
that is preserved by the optimal trajectories, whatever the choice of the initial condition.

However, the
actual
benefit
may
be rather limited from a practical point of view,
particularly
if the definition
of such a set
{\mathcal{B}}
is not
sufficiently
explicit: for
example
,
such a domain
may be difficult to
deal with
from a numerical point of view if it is not clearly (or simply) defined.
Instead, we thus propose a cheaper strategy, which we plan to address in detail in a future work.
We
argue
that from a practical point of view,
it is indeed
sufficient
to choose

{\mathcal{B}}

as the subset of a wider
set
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\mathscr{O}}_{N}

such that the boundary
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
is unreachable from
{\mathcal{B}}
, and this whatever the choice
of the strategy
\alpha\in{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
satisfying
(
3.36
)
. In our setting, we can revisit
Lemma

3.15
,
Propositions

3.18

and

3.20

quite easily
to get the following

reformulation of Lemma

3.26

(in order to ease the presentation, we directly write down the
new
statement
for the
finite-dimensional
version formulated in
(
3.35
), but there is no difficulty in formulating
a similar statement for
(
2.5
)):

Lemma 3.27
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be in force. Then,

for any non-negative constants
c
,
C
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
there exist two constants
\tilde{\gamma}\in(0,1)
and

\tilde{c}\geq 0
and an integer
\widetilde{N}
,
with the following property:
for any
initial condition
(t,m)\in\mathcal{P}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
with
\|m\|_{2,q-1}^{2}\leq c
and
m\geq\gamma
,
for any
N\geq\widetilde{N}

and
for any measurable function
\alpha:[t,T]\rightarrow{\mathscr{A}}_{N}
satisfying
(
3.36
)
with respect to
C
, the
solution

(\xi_{s}^{N})_{t\leq s\leq T}

to
(
3.34
) (with
{\mathbf{z}}=\{\widehat{m}(k)\}_{|k|\leq N}
)
satisfies

\sum_{|k|\leq N}\xi_{s}^{N}(k)e_{k}\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{\gamma}},\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{|k|\leq N}|\xi_{s}^{N}(k)|^{2}\bigl(1+|k|^{2}\bigr)^{2\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}\leq\tilde{c}}.

As we already explained in the previous subsection,
C
right above must be sufficiently large so that

\alpha^{*,N}
, which already belongs to
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
, also satisfies
(
3.36
).

Lemma
3.27

says that we can put for free an additional reflection (or absorption) term on the boundary of
{\mathscr{O}}_{N}
or on the boundary of any subset

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\mathscr{O}}_{N}
containing
{\mathcal{B}}:=\{{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathscr{O}}_{N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{|k|\leq N}|z_{k}|^{2}(1+|k|^{2})^{q-1}\leq{c}\ {\rm and}\ \sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}\geq{\gamma}}\}
.

Given such a subset

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
, we can indeed
follow
the definition
(
3.35
)
and then
consider the optimal control problem

(3.38)

\inf\Gamma^{N}\left(\alpha,t,\textbf{z}\right),\quad(t,\mathbf{z})\in[0,T]\times\widetilde{\mathcal{B}},

the infimum being taken
over controls

\alpha\in{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
satisfying
(
3.36
), but
(differently from
(
3.35
))
the corresponding controlled trajectories

(\{\xi^{N}_{t}(k)\}_{|k|\leq N})_{0\leq t\leq T}

in
(
3.34
)
being reflected on the boundary of

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
(or being absorbed, with then an appropriate extension of the definition of the cost functional

\Gamma^{N}
to cover the case when absorption occurs).
From Lemma
3.27
, we notice that
for

{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, the
controlled trajectories starting from
{\mathbf{z}}
are
never reflected (nor absorbed). As a result, the
value function associated with
(
3.38
)
and denoted
\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{N}

coincides
with
\Upsilon^{N}
on
[0,T]\times{\mathcal{B}}
: this gives a way to access

\Upsilon^{N}
through a finite-dimensional control problem with
a properly defined boundary condition.
Typically, we can choose the domain
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
as the intersection of
an ellipsoid and
a polyhedron:

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}&:=\Bigl\{{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{(2N+1)^{d}}:\sum_{|k|\leq N}|z_{k}|^{2}(1+|k|^{2})^{\lfloor q-2-d/2\rfloor}\leq\tilde{c}\Bigr\}\cap\biggl(\bigcap_{|k|\leq N}\Bigl\{{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{(2N+1)^{d}}:z_{-k}=\overline{z}_{k}\Bigr\}\biggr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\cap\bigl\{{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{(2N+1)^{d}}:z_{0}=1\bigr\}\cap\biggl(\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}\Bigl\{{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{(2N+1)^{d}}:\sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}(x_{i})\geq\tilde{\gamma}\Bigr\}\biggr),\end{split}

with
(x_{i})_{i=1,\dots,m}

forming a
\delta
-net of
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
for some
\delta>0
(i.e.,
the union of the balls of center
x_{i}
, for
i=1,\cdots,m
, and radius

\delta
covers
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
).
It is clear that
{\mathcal{B}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
(since
c\leq\tilde{c}
and
\gamma\geq\tilde{\gamma}
).
The only difficulty (with this choice) is thus to check that

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
is included in
{\mathscr{O}}_{N}
, which we can prove as follows.
For
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, we obviously have

\sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}(x)\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
To prove that the latter is non-negative, we notice that there exists
i\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
such that
|x-x_{i}|\leq\delta
and then
obtain for

{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
,

\begin{split}\sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}(x)\geq\sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}(x_{i})-\sum_{|k|\leq N}|z_{k}||e_{k}(x_{i})-e_{k}(x)|&\geq\tilde{\gamma}-2\pi\delta\sum_{|k|\leq N}|z_{k}||k|\\
&\geq\tilde{\gamma}-2C_{\tilde{c},q}\pi\delta,\end{split}

for a constant
C_{\tilde{c},q}
depending on
\tilde{c}
,
q
(and
d
). For
\delta
small, the right-hand side is strictly positive.

The
control problem
(
3.38
)
with
a reflection (or an absorption) condition
leads to an HJB equation with a Neumann (or Dirichlet) boundary condition,
and the value function

\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{N}

of this new control problem with reflection (or absorption) coincides
with

\Upsilon^{N}

in
(
3.35
),
at least
on
[0,T]\times{\mathcal{B}}
.
On the finite-dimensional space
[0,T]\times\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
,
the corresponding HJB equation
formally writes:

(3.39)

\begin{cases}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\partial_{t}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{N}(t,\mathbf{z})-\sum_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}\lvert 2\pi k\rvert^{2}z_{k}\partial_{z_{k}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{N}(t,\mathbf{z})\\
\quad\quad-\sum_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}z_{k}\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-73.28773pt\hbox{$\check{H}^{N}\left(\cdot,\mathrm{i}2\pi\sum_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}j\partial_{z_{j}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{N}(t,\textbf{z})e_{j}(\cdot)\right)$}\hskip-73.28773pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{19.41238pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(k)+\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{z})=0,\\
\quad\\
\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{N}(T,\textbf{z})=\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{z}).\end{cases}

where, with a small abuse of notation, we have written
\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{z})=\mathcal{G}(\sum_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z_{k}}e_{k}(\cdot))
and
\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{z})=\mathcal{F}(\sum_{\lvert k\rvert\leq N}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z_{k}}e_{k}(\cdot))
.
Here, the notation

\partial_{z_{k}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{N}(t,\mathbf{z})

is rather abusive. It is not a complex (holomorphic) derivative but just a vector of two real derivatives:

\begin{split}\partial_{z_{k}}&:=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\Re[z_{k}]}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\partial_{\Im[z_{k}]}.\end{split}

As for
\check{H}^{N}
, it is a new Hamiltonian, whose expression must take into account the definition of

{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
together with the condition
(
3.36
) in the statement of Lemma

3.26
, namely

\begin{split}\check{H}^{N}(x,p)&:=\sup\biggl\{-L\biggl(x,-D_{p}H\Bigl(x,\textrm{\rm i}2\pi\sum_{|l|\leq N}l\zeta(l)e_{l}(x)\Bigr)\biggr)+D_{p}H\Bigl(x,\textrm{\rm i}2\pi\sum_{|l|\leq N}l\zeta(l)e_{l}(x)\Bigr)\cdot p\,;\\
&\hskip 45.0pt\zeta(l)\in{\mathbb{C}}:\overline{\zeta(l)}=\zeta(-l),\ \sum_{|l|\leq N}|l|^{2d+6}|\zeta(l)|^{2}\leq C\biggr\}.\end{split}

When
p
itself can be written in the form

p=\textrm{\rm i}2\pi\sum_{|l|\leq N}l\zeta(l)e_{l}(x)
for
\{\zeta(l)\}_{|l|\leq N}
as above, it holds

\check{H}^{N}(x,p)=H(x,p)
.
Of course, so should be the case when

p=\mathrm{i}2\pi\sum_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}j\partial_{z_{j}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{N}(t,\textbf{z})e_{j}(x)

for
(t,{\mathbf{z}})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{B}}
, in which case

p
should be also equal to

\mathrm{i}2\pi\sum_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}j\partial_{z_{j}}\Upsilon^{N}(t,\textbf{z})e_{j}(x)
.
This should permit to identify
(
3.39
) on

[0,T]\times{\mathcal{B}}

with
(
3.37
).
For simplicity, we feel better not to give the full proof of
this claim, but it does not raise any main difficulty. In short,
the point is to prove that

(3.40)

\sum_{|j|\leq N}|j|^{2d+6}|\partial_{z_{j}}\Upsilon^{N}(t,\textbf{z})|^{2}\leq C,

for
{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathcal{B}}
.
Intuitively, Pontryagin’s principle provides a
representation of

\partial_{z_{j}}\Upsilon^{N}(t,\textbf{z})
, but
for

{\mathbf{z}}
in

{\mathcal{B}}
,
the Pontryagin system may be identified with
(

⁢
F

B
N

)
and in turn the quantity

\mathrm{i}2\pi\sum_{\lvert j\rvert\leq N}j\partial_{z_{j}}\Upsilon^{N}(t,\textbf{z})e_{j}(x)

may be identified
with

\nabla u^{N}_{t}(x)
(when
(

⁢
F

B
N

)
is initialized from

m=\sum_{|k|\leq N}z_{k}e_{k}
at time
t
).
Display
(
3.40
)
then follows from the choice of
C
,
as we assumed the latter to be sufficiently large so that

\alpha^{*,N}
satisfies
(
3.36
)
(under the additional condition that

q\geq 3d/2+4
).

Again, we plan to elaborate on
(
3.39
)
in future works dedicated to numerical applications.
Conceptually,
(
3.39
)
should be seen as a finite-dimensional reformulation
(up to the change of Hamiltonian)
of
(
3.37
).
The reader is referred to

[
13
]

for more details on the
Fourier formulation of PDEs set on
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

and to

[
30
]
for more details on control problems with Neumann boundary conditions.

3.4.4.
Connection with mean field games

The forward-backward system
(

⁢
F

B
N

)
can be regarded as the mean field game system of a (so-called potential) mean field game admitting

\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{F}}
and
\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{G}}
as respective running and terminal costs, see
for instance

[
8
, Chapter 3.7]
. Subsequently,
the system
(

⁢
F

B
N

)
can be
regarded as a Fourier-Galerkin approximation of this mean field game system.
In fact, it may be easily seen that
the specific structure of the running and terminal costs as derivatives of functions
defined on
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
